I remember in grade school getting in trouble for popping out of my seat and talking over the teacher. I’d also rush ahead before the directions were even finished. My mother used to say I acted like I was wound too tight, even back then.\n\nDoctor: Did you often interrupt others or blurt out answers before they finished speaking when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, I did. I’d blurt things out before the teacher or whoever was talking finished. Even now I catch myself doing it—like if my supervisor or someone at church is giving instructions, I’ll jump in with what I think they’re about to say, and sometimes I’m wrong and they have to correct me.\n\nDoctor: Did you often get into physical fights or engage in risky behaviors as a child?\n\nPatient: I don’t really remember that being a big thing. I wasn’t getting into fights all the time or doing dangerous stuff, at least not that I can clearly point to. My trouble was more with being restless and speaking before thinking.\n\nDoctor: Did you often have difficulty paying attention to details or making careless mistakes in your work or activities as a child?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure about that one. I don’t remember being told I made a lot of careless mistakes or missed details as a kid. That wasn’t something that stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out answers before they finished speaking in your current daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes, I still do that. Like when my supervisor or someone from the church staff is giving me instructions, I’ll jump in with what I think they’re about to say before they finish. Sometimes I’m wrong, and then they have to stop and correct me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

It feels like there’s a motor running under my skin, especially in staff meetings or briefings at church. I’ll start shifting around, standing up, or finding some reason to get up and move. It’s been bothering me enough that I figured I should talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience these feelings of restlessness when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, I did. I remember in grade school getting in trouble for popping out of my seat and talking over the teacher. I’d also rush ahead before the directions were even finished. My mother used to say I acted like I was wound too tight, even back then.\n\nDoctor: Did you often interrupt others or blurt out answers before they finished speaking when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, I did. I’d blurt things out before the teacher or whoever was talking finished. Even now I catch myself doing it—like if my supervisor or someone at church is giving instructions, I’ll jump in with what I think they’re about to say, and sometimes I’m wrong and they have to correct me.\n\nDoctor: Did you often get into physical fights or engage in risky behaviors as a child?\n\nPatient: I don’t really remember that being a big thing. I wasn’t getting into fights all the time or doing dangerous stuff, at least not that I can clearly point to. My trouble was more with being restless and speaking before thinking.\n\nDoctor: Did you often have difficulty paying attention to details or making careless mistakes in your work or activities as a child?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure about that one. I don’t remember being told I made a lot of careless mistakes or missed details as a kid. That wasn’t something that stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out answers before they finished speaking in your current daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes, I still do that. Like when my supervisor or someone from the church staff is giving me instructions, I’ll jump in with what I think they’re about to say before they finish. Sometimes I’m wrong, and then they have to stop and correct me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse in your daily life, doing things without fully thinking them through?\n\nPatient: Yes, I guess I do in that way. Like with instructions, I’ll jump in before the person is done talking because I think I already know where they’re going. Then sometimes I’m wrong, and my supervisor or someone at the church has to stop and correct me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty paying attention to details or making careless mistakes in your work or daily activities now?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that’s a big issue for me now. I don’t really notice making a lot of careless mistakes or missing details in my work. If it happens, it’s not something that stands out to me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I’m here because I can’t seem to sit still when I’m supposed to. It feels like there’s a motor running under my skin, especially in staff meetings or briefings at church. I’ll start shifting around, standing up, or finding some reason to get up and move. It’s been bothering me enough that I figured I should talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience these feelings of restlessness when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, I did. I remember in grade school getting in trouble for popping out of my seat and talking over the teacher. I’d also rush ahead before the directions were even finished. My mother used to say I acted like I was wound too tight, even back then.\n\nDoctor: Did you often interrupt others or blurt out answers before they finished speaking when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, I did. I’d blurt things out before the teacher or whoever was talking finished. Even now I catch myself doing it—like if my supervisor or someone at church is giving instructions, I’ll jump in with what I think they’re about to say, and sometimes I’m wrong and they have to correct me.\n\nDoctor: Did you often get into physical fights or engage in risky behaviors as a child?\n\nPatient: I don’t really remember that being a big thing. I wasn’t getting into fights all the time or doing dangerous stuff, at least not that I can clearly point to. My trouble was more with being restless and speaking before thinking.\n\nDoctor: Did you often have difficulty paying attention to details or making careless mistakes in your work or activities as a child?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure about that one. I don’t remember being told I made a lot of careless mistakes or missed details as a kid. That wasn’t something that stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out answers before they finished speaking in your current daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes, I still do that. Like when my supervisor or someone from the church staff is giving me instructions, I’ll jump in with what I think they’re about to say before they finish. Sometimes I’m wrong, and then they have to stop and correct me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse in your daily life, doing things without fully thinking them through?\n\nPatient: Yes, I guess I do in that way. Like with instructions, I’ll jump in before the person is done talking because I think I already know where they’re going. Then sometimes I’m wrong, and my supervisor or someone at the church has to stop and correct me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty paying attention to details or making careless mistakes in your work or daily activities now?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that’s a big issue for me now. I don’t really notice making a lot of careless mistakes or missing details in my work. If it happens, it’s not something that stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble organizing tasks and activities in your daily life?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed that as a major problem day to day. I usually just do what needs to be done, but I don’t know if I’d call it trouble organizing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I can get sidetracked sometimes, sure, but I haven’t noticed it being a constant thing where everything around me pulls my attention away.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble sustaining focus during tasks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I can usually stay with a task if I know what I’m doing, especially at work. I might drift a little sometimes, but I haven’t really noticed a big problem with keeping focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you often struggle to follow through on instructions or finish tasks at work or in other activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that happens often. If someone gives me instructions, I usually try to get it done. I mean, things can get a little messy sometimes, but I usually know where things are and I clean up when I need to. It’s like I can’t quite settle down and just enjoy it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your restlessness and impulsive behaviors are causing problems in your relationships or at work?\n\nPatient: Yes, mostly at work. It’s started causing some problems there. I’ll miss part of what someone told me to do, or I’ll jump into decisions about supplies or room turnovers too fast, and then it creates friction with coworkers. My supervisor has had to correct me a few times, and he actually suggested I go through the employee assistance program. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I’m here because I can’t seem to sit still when I’m supposed to. It feels like there’s a motor running under my skin, especially in staff meetings or briefings at church. I’ll start shifting around, standing up, or finding some reason to get up and move. It’s been bothering me enough that I figured I should talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience these feelings of restlessness when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, I did. I remember in grade school getting in trouble for popping out of my seat and talking over the teacher. I’d also rush ahead before the directions were even finished. My mother used to say I acted like I was wound too tight, even back then.\n\nDoctor: Did you often interrupt others or blurt out answers before they finished speaking when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, I did. I’d blurt things out before the teacher or whoever was talking finished. Even now I catch myself doing it—like if my supervisor or someone at church is giving instructions, I’ll jump in with what I think they’re about to say, and sometimes I’m wrong and they have to correct me.\n\nDoctor: Did you often get into physical fights or engage in risky behaviors as a child?\n\nPatient: I don’t really remember that being a big thing. I wasn’t getting into fights all the time or doing dangerous stuff, at least not that I can clearly point to. My trouble was more with being restless and speaking before thinking.\n\nDoctor: Did you often have difficulty paying attention to details or making careless mistakes in your work or activities as a child?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure about that one. I don’t remember being told I made a lot of careless mistakes or missed details as a kid. That wasn’t something that stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out answers before they finished speaking in your current daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes, I still do that. Like when my supervisor or someone from the church staff is giving me instructions, I’ll jump in with what I think they’re about to say before they finish. Sometimes I’m wrong, and then they have to stop and correct me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse in your daily life, doing things without fully thinking them through?\n\nPatient: Yes, I guess I do in that way. Like with instructions, I’ll jump in before the person is done talking because I think I already know where they’re going. Then sometimes I’m wrong, and my supervisor or someone at the church has to stop and correct me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty paying attention to details or making careless mistakes in your work or daily activities now?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that’s a big issue for me now. I don’t really notice making a lot of careless mistakes or missing details in my work. If it happens, it’s not something that stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble organizing tasks and activities in your daily life?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed that as a major problem day to day. I usually just do what needs to be done, but I don’t know if I’d call it trouble organizing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I can get sidetracked sometimes, sure, but I haven’t noticed it being a constant thing where everything around me pulls my attention away.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble sustaining focus during tasks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I can usually stay with a task if I know what I’m doing, especially at work. I might drift a little sometimes, but I haven’t really noticed a big problem with keeping focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you often struggle to follow through on instructions or finish tasks at work or in other activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that happens often. If someone gives me instructions, I usually try to get it done. I might need to double-check what they wanted sometimes, but I haven’t really noticed a big problem with not finishing tasks.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty keeping your workspace or living space organized?\n\nPatient: I don’t think that’s a big issue for me. I’ll miss part of what someone told me to do, or I’ll jump into decisions about supplies or room turnovers too fast, and then it creates friction with coworkers. I remember in grade school getting in trouble for popping out of my seat and talking over the teacher. I’d also rush ahead before the directions were even finished. I’d blurt things out before the teacher or whoever was talking finished. I wasn’t getting into fights all the time or doing dangerous stuff, at least not that I can clearly point to. I don’t remember being told I made a lot of careless mistakes or missed details as a kid. Like when my supervisor or someone from the church staff is giving me instructions, I’ll jump in with what I think they’re about to say before they finish. Sometimes I’m wrong, and then they have to stop and correct me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse in your daily life, doing things without fully thinking them through?\n\nPatient: Yes, I guess I do in that way. Like with instructions, I’ll jump in before the person is done talking because I think I already know where they’re going. Then sometimes I’m wrong, and my supervisor or someone at the church has to stop and correct me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty paying attention to details or making careless mistakes in your work or daily activities now?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that’s a big issue for me now. I don’t really notice making a lot of careless mistakes or missing details in my work. If it happens, it’s not something that stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble organizing tasks and activities in your daily life?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed that as a major problem day to day. I usually just do what needs to be done, but I don’t know if I’d call it trouble organizing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I can get sidetracked sometimes, sure, but I haven’t noticed it being a constant thing where everything around me pulls my attention away.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble sustaining focus during tasks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. My supervisor has had to correct me a few times, and he actually suggested I go through the employee assistance program. I mean, I’ve probably always been a bit restless, but this worse stretch has been going on about a year and five months. It started after my cleaning contract changed to rotating hours and more event work.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with any other mental health conditions?\n\nPatient: No, not that I know of. I haven’t really been diagnosed with anything else mental health-wise.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If someone gives me instructions, I usually try to get it done. I mean, things can get a little messy sometimes, but I usually know where things are and I clean up when I need to. I start shifting around, or I’ll stand up, or I’ll look for some reason to move around. Even at home or when I’m supposed to be relaxing, I have a hard time just sitting still and being quiet.
